=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two integers n and m that consist of the same number of digits.
You can perform the following operations any number of times:

Choose any digit from n that is not 9 and increase it by 1.
Choose any digit from n that is not 0 and decrease it by 1.

The integer n must not be a prime number at any point, including its original value and after each operation.
The cost of a transformation is the sum of all values that n takes throughout the operations performed.
Return the minimum cost to transform n into m. If it is impossible, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 10, m = 12
Output: 85
Explanation:
We perform the following operations:

Increase the first digit, now n = 20.
Increase the second digit, now n = 21.
Increase the second digit, now n = 22.
Decrease the first digit, now n = 12.


Example 2:

Input: n = 4, m = 8
Output: -1
Explanation:
It is impossible to make n equal to m.

Example 3:

Input: n = 6, m = 2
Output: -1
Explanation: 
Since 2 is already a prime, we can't make n equal to m.

 
Constraints:

1 <= n, m < 10^4
n and m consist of the same number of digits.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, n: int, m: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

def neighbors(x):
    s = str(x)
    for i,ch in enumerate(s):
        if ch < '9': yield int(s[:i]+str(int(ch)+1)+s[i+1:])
        if ch > '0': yield int(s[:i]+str(int(ch)-1)+s[i+1:])
```

Pseudo:
```
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, n: int, m: int) -> int:
        if n == m:
            return n if not self.is_prime[n] else -1
        if is_prime[n] or is_prime[m]: return -1? Actually if m prime, Dijkstra won't reach; can early return -1. If n prime return -1.
        d = len(str(n))
        # sieve up to 9999
        N = 10000
        is_prime = [True]*N
        is_prime[0]=is_prime[1]=False
        for i in range(2,int(N**0.5)+1):
            if is_prime[i]:
                for j in range(i*i,N,i):
                    is_prime[j]=False
        if is_prime[n] or is_prime[m]: return -1 # m prime impossible
        INF = 10**18
        dist = [INF]*10000
        dist[n]=n
        heap=[(n,n)]
        while heap:
            cost,u=heappop
            if cost != dist[u]: continue
            if u == m: return cost
            s = str(u)
            # ensure length d? u always d digits because first digit nonzero. For d=1, str length1.
            for i,ch in enumerate(s):
                digit = ord(ch)-48
                if digit < 9:
                    v = int(s[:i] + chr(digit+49) + s[i+1:])
                    if not is_prime[v]:
                        nd = cost + v
                        if nd < dist[v]:
                            dist[v]=nd; heappush
                if digit > 0:
                    if i == 0 and digit == 1:
                        continue
                    v = int(s[:i] + chr(digit+47) + s[i+1:])
                    if not is_prime[v]:
                        nd = cost + v
                        if nd < dist[v]:
                            dist[v]=nd; heappush
        return -1
```

Now, let's think about if "n and m consist of same number of digits" but n,m <10^4, so d=len(str(n)). If n=10,m=12, d=2. If n=1000,m=1001, d=4. Good.

Now, let's think about if m could be prime but n=m? We return -1. If m prime and n nonprime, early return -1. Is it possible to "transform n into m" where m prime if final value prime is not allowed? No, "including original value and after each operation" final after last operation is after operation, so must not be prime. So impossible. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can use a simple DP because graph is acyclic? No, graph has cycles (increment/decrement). Dijkstra.

Now, let's think about if we should include original n in dist init but if n prime return -1. Good.

Now, let's think about if we should check m prime before n prime? If n prime and m prime, return -1. If n nonprime m prime, return -1. If n prime m nonprime, return -1. ```
if is_prime[n] or is_prime[m]: return -1
if n == m: return n
```

Now, let's think about if n=10,m=12, could path 10->0->1->10->20... include 0? Under integer semantics, 0 dead but could go back to10? Wait from 0, increase digit to1 ->1. From 1, cannot go to10. So 0->1 dead. Actually 0 cannot go to10 because no tens digit. So 10->0 cannot return to10. Thus any path with leading zero drop cannot reach m. Good. Under zfill, 0 can return to10. But we reject.

Now, let's think about if n=5244,m=5244. answer5244. Good.

Now, let's think about if n=5245,m=5245. answer5245. Good.

Now, let's think about if n=5246,m=5246. answer
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, n: int, m: int) -> int:
        # Sieve primes up to 9999.
        N = 10000
        is_prime = [True] * N
        is_prime[0] = is_prime[1] = False
        for i in range(2, int(N ** 0.5) + 1):
            if is_prime[i]:
                for j in range(i * i, N, i):
                    is_prime[j] = False

        # The original and final values must not be prime.
        if is_prime[n] or is_prime[m]:
            return -1

        import heapq

        INF = 10**18
        dist = [INF] * N
        dist[n] = n
        pq = [(n, n)]

        while pq:
            cost, x = heapq.heappop(pq)
            if cost != dist[x]:
                continue
            if x == m:
                return cost

            s = str(x)
            d = len(s)

            for i, ch in enumerate(s):
                digit = ord(ch) - 48

                # Increase digit by 1.
                if digit < 9:
                    y = int(s[:i] + chr(digit + 49) + s[i+1:])
                    if not is_prime[y]:
                        nd = cost + y
                        if nd < dist[y]:
                            dist[y] = nd
                            heapq.heappush(pq, (nd, y))

                # Decrease digit by 1.
                if digit > 0:
                    # The leading digit cannot become 0, because then the
                    # number would lose a digit and could never regain it.
                    if i == 0 and digit == 1:
                        continue
                    y = int(s[:i] + chr(digit + 47) + s[i+1:])
                    if not is_prime[y]:
                        nd = cost + y
                        if nd < dist[y]:
                            dist[y] = nd
                            heapq.heappush(pq, (nd, y))

        return -1
```
